Well, I... I don't want to make it sound like it's taking over my life or anything. I try really hard to just push it aside and keep busy. I don't want to be one of those folks who just sits around feeling sorry for themselves, you know?

name: Negative_Beliefs\n  description: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.\n  manifestation: I know it may sound foolish, but now I keep thinking everyday things aren't safe and that if I trust something simple, it'll turn on me.\n- key: S075\n  name: Social_Detachment\n  description: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.\n  manifestation: My wife will be sitting right there and the bridge fellows will be joking around, but I feel like I'm behind a window and not really with them.\n- key: S076\n  name: Inability_Positive_Emotions\n  description: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).\n  manifestation: I still water the garden and tinker with old radios some, but the good feeling I used to get from it just doesn't come like it did.\n- key: S077\n  name: Irritability_Aggression\n  description: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.\n  manifestation: I get short with people over small things, like my wife moving something in the garage, and then I feel bad because I know she didn't do anything wrong.\n- key: S079\n  name: Hypervigilance\n  description: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.\n  manifestation: When I go places now I look around for what might fall, where the exits are, and whether the floor or steps look steady before I can sit easy.\n- key: S080\n  name: Exaggerated_Startle_Response\n  description: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.\n  manifestation: If a door bangs or someone comes up behind me, I jump hard and my chest keeps thumping for a while even after I know it's nothing.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I still manage my basic routines, but I’ve stopped going to the senior center and I avoid enough things around the house that my wife has to handle chores I used to do. I also don’t visit with my bridge friends much anymore, so my days have gotten smaller.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 7 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on since the ladder accident about a year and seven months ago.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 74 / not_in_workforce / less_than_9th\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S027\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: How upsetting are these memories of the accident when they come back to you?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I don't want to make it sound like it's taking over my life or anything. I try really hard to just push it aside and keep busy. I don't want to be one of those folks who just sits around feeling sorry for themselves, you know? \n\nBut... well, sometimes it does catch me off guard. If I hear a certain noise, like... like metal scraping against concrete, or even if I just see a ladder leaning up against a house when I'm out for a walk... it's like it all just jumps right back into my head. Just for a second, it feels like I'm right back under there, waiting and hoping for someone to come help me. \n\nIt shakes me up a little bit, I suppose, but I always try to just take a deep breath and move on. I hope that doesn't sound too dramatic, Doctor. I'm really trying my best to handle it well... does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: How would you describe the level of distress you feel when these memories of the accident come back to you?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... I'm not rightly sure how to put a measurement on it, to be honest with you. 'Distress' sounds like a pretty strong word, and I... I promise I'm not falling apart or anything like that. \n\nIt's just... a bit bothersome, I suppose? I wouldn't call it a high level at all. I just get a little rattled for a moment and then I go find something to keep my hands busy. I really don't want to make a mountain out of a molehill here, so I wouldn't say it's anything too terrible. \n\nI hope that's an okay way to explain it... I just don't really know how to grade it for you, Doctor. Is that alright?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself trying not to think about the accident with the ladder?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say I actively try not to think about it. I mean, I don't sit there telling myself, \"Don't think about the ladder, don't think about the ladder.\" I just... I prefer to keep my mind on more pleasant things, you know? \n\nI've got my garden, and I like to watch the birds, so I just focus on that instead. It's not really avoiding it, it's just... choosing to be positive. I don't want to be the kind of person who dwells on the bad stuff. I try to be cheerful. \n\nDoes that sound like the right way to handle it, Doctor? I just want to make sure I'm doing what I'm supposed to be doing.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often feel on edge or easily startled?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I... I wouldn't say I'm always on edge. I try to stay real steady, you know? I don't want you to think I'm just a nervous wreck all the time. 

But, I suppose... well, if a door bangs shut real sudden-like, or if somebody comes up behind me without me hearing 'em... I do tend to jump pretty hard. And then my chest, it just keeps thumping and thumping for a good while. Even after I turn around and I know it's absolutely nothing to worry about, my heart just keeps racing. 

I always feel a bit silly when it happens, to be honest with you. I try to just laugh it off so folks don't think I'm getting frail or overly spooky. I hope that doesn't sound too bad, Doctor. It's just a reflex, I think... is that a normal thing to happen?

Well, like I was saying before, I don't really sit around trying not to think about it... but I suppose I have been, well, finding little excuses to stay away from certain spots. 

I just haven't felt much like going down to the senior center lately, and... well, I've been keeping out of the garage, too. I find other things that need doing inside the house instead. 

I just figure it's better to keep busy where I'm comfortable, rather than go looking for trouble. That makes sense, doesn't it, Doctor? I just want to make sure I'm going about this the right way.

Well, I... I wouldn't say I've stopped doing things just because of the ladder. I mean, I still get up and do my basic routines every day. I make my coffee, I read the paper... I'm doing just fine with all that. I don't want you to think I'm just sitting around wasting away.

I lie there a long time at night. And then, when I finally do drop off, I end up waking up from... well, bad dreams, or even just little noises in the house. Once I'm up, I just can't seem to settle back down for the life of me. Is that... does that make sense, Doctor? I hope I'm answering your question alright.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel tired or sluggish during the day?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... no, not really. I mean, I try to keep myself busy enough during the day, you know? I don't want to sound like I'm just sitting around complaining. \n\nI might rest in my chair for a little bit in the afternoon, but... well, at my age, I think taking a little breather is just normal, isn't it? I wouldn't say I feel sluggish, no sir. I try to keep moving so I'm not a bother to anyone. I hope that's... I hope that's the kind of answer you were looking for, Doctor?\n\nDoctor: You mentioned waking up from bad dreams, can you tell me more about these bad dreams?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... I feel a bit foolish even making a fuss over them, to be honest. I don't want to make a mountain out of a molehill. But... they're mostly about this... well, a bad spot I was in a ways back. In the dreams, I'm stuck. \n\nAnd... well, if I'm being completely honest with you, Doctor, it's not always just when I'm asleep. Sometimes, if I'm out and I hear a piece of metal scrape real loud, or even just see a ladder leaning up in somebody's yard... the whole thing just jumps right back into my head. It feels exactly like I'm right back under there again, just waiting for help. \n\nI try to just shake it off, you know, so I don't bother anyone with it. I hope I'm not sounding crazy saying all that... is that what you were asking about, Doctor?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places or activities because they remind you of the bad spot you were in?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I suppose maybe a little bit, but I really try not to let it get in the way of things. I don't want to be a nuisance or seem like I'm hiding away. \n\nBut, if I'm being perfectly honest... I do find myself making up little excuses not to go down to the senior center as much lately. And... well, my garage. I used to like tinkering out there, but now I just... I kind of steer clear of it. Especially over by where I keep the tools, and that corner where the ladder used to sit. It just feels better to stay inside, you know? \n\nI hope that doesn't make me sound too silly, Doctor. I just figure it's easier to avoid the fuss. Does that... does that answer your question alright?\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these bad dreams and trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I guess it's been a while now. I really thought it would just sort of fade away on its own, you know? I didn't want to make a big deal out of it or bother anyone. \n\nBut, um... if I'm thinking back on it, it's been going on since that accident I had with the ladder. That was... oh, about a year and seven months ago now. I kept hoping a good night's rest would just sort it all out, but it just seems to have stuck around. I hope I didn't wait too long to say something about it, Doctor... is that alright?\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided talking about the accident with the ladder to anyone?\n\nPatient: Oh, no... no, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I don't go around bringing it up out of the blue, but that's mostly just because... well, I don't want to be a bother or bore folks with my troubles. I always try to be good company, you know? \n\nBut if my daughter asks how I'm doing, or... or like you asking me about it right now, I don't mind answering at all. I certainly don't want to be difficult or hide anything from you, Doctor. I just try my best not to complain too much to people. \n\nIs it alright that I handle it that way? I hope I'm giving you the right answers.\n\nDoctor: How upsetting are these memories of the accident when they come back to you?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I don't want to make it sound like it's taking over my life or anything. I try really hard to just push it aside and keep busy. I don't want to be one of those folks who just sits around feeling sorry for themselves, you know? \n\nBut... well, sometimes it does catch me off guard. If I hear a certain noise, like... like metal scraping against concrete, or even if I just see a ladder leaning up against a house when I'm out for a walk... it's like it all just jumps right back into my head. Just for a second, it feels like I'm right back under there, waiting and hoping for someone to come help me. \n\nIt shakes me up a little bit, I suppose, but I always try to just take a deep breath and move on. I hope that doesn't sound too dramatic, Doctor. I'm really trying my best to handle it well... does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: How would you describe the level of distress you feel when these memories of the accident come back to you?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... I'm not rightly sure how to put a measurement on it, to be honest with you. 'Distress' sounds like a pretty strong word, and I... I promise I'm not falling apart or anything like that. \n\nIt's just... a bit bothersome, I suppose? I wouldn't call it a high level at all. I just get a little rattled for a moment and then I go find something to keep my hands busy. I really don't want to make a mountain out of a molehill here, so I wouldn't say it's anything too terrible. \n\nI hope that's an okay way to explain it... I just don't really know how to grade it for you, Doctor. Is that alright?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself trying not to think about the accident with the ladder?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say I actively try not to think about it. I mean, I don't sit there telling myself, \"Don't think about the ladder, don't think about the ladder.\" I just... I prefer to keep my mind on more pleasant things, you know? \n\nI've got my garden, and I like to watch the birds, so I just focus on that instead. It's not really avoiding it, it's just... choosing to be positive. I don't want to be the kind of person who dwells on the bad stuff. I try to be cheerful. \n\nDoes that sound like the right way to handle it, Doctor? I just want to make sure I'm doing what I'm supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel on edge or easily startled?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I wouldn't say I'm always on edge. I do tend to jump pretty hard. And then my chest, it just keeps thumping and thumping for a good while. Even after I turn around and I know it's absolutely nothing to worry about, my heart just keeps racing. \n\nI always feel a bit silly when it happens, to be honest with you. I try to just laugh it off so folks don't think I'm getting frail or overly spooky. I hope that doesn't sound too bad, Doctor. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Well, no, not really. I mean, like I was saying, it's mostly just the senior center and the garage. I just... I keep finding little reasons not to go to the center, and I stay out of the garage, especially over by the tools and that one spot where I used to keep the ladder. 

But other than that, I'm really trying to keep to my normal routine, I promise. I don't want to be a bother or make it seem like I'm hiding from the world. It's just those couple of places, mostly. That's not too bad, is it, Doctor? I'm doing okay, considering?

I always try to be good company, you know? \n\nBut if my daughter asks how I'm doing, or... or like you asking me about it right now, I don't mind answering at all. I hope I'm giving you the right answers.\n\nDoctor: How upsetting are these memories of the accident when they come back to you?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I don't want to make it sound like it's taking over my life or anything. I try really hard to just push it aside and keep busy. I don't want to be a bother or make it seem like I'm hiding from the world. It's just those couple of places, mostly. That's not too bad, is it, Doctor? I'm doing okay, considering?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself trying to push away or avoid feeling the emotions that come up when you think about the accident with the ladder?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I think we might have just talked about that a little bit, didn't we? I really don't try to push those feelings away, I promise. \n\nIf I start feeling a bit uneasy or... or sad about the fall, I just try to sit with it for a minute until it passes. I don't want you to think I'm ignoring it or trying to pretend it didn't happen. I know you doctors always say it's important to face things, and I really am trying my best to follow that advice. \n\nI'm just letting things come naturally, like we discussed. That's still the right way to go about it, isn't it? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right answers and doing what I'm supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding social interactions or conversations with others because they might ask about the accident?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... no, I don't think I'm avoiding people. I mean, I love chatting with folks, I really do. It's just... well, like I mentioned before, I've just been finding little excuses not to go down to the senior center lately. And I haven't been tinkering around in the garage much, especially not over by the tools or where that ladder used to be. \n\nBut it's not because I don't want to talk to anyone! I just... I guess I'd rather not get into the whole story of the fall right now, you know? It's easier just to stay home and putter around the house. That makes sense, doesn't it, Doctor? It's okay to just want a little quiet time to myself, right? I'm not doing anything wrong by staying in a bit more?\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you find yourself avoiding conversations about the accident with the ladder because it reminds you of what happened?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 74 / not_in_workforce / less_than_9th\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

    "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I think... I think that's sort of what we were just touching on, right? I really don't try to avoid talking about it because of the memories. 

I mean, if someone comes right out and asks me about the ladder, I'll answer them. I don't just plug my ears or walk away or anything rude like that. I know the fall happened, and I'm not trying to pretend it didn't. I just... I just don't go out of my way to bring up the whole story if I don't have to, like I was saying. 

But I'm certainly not scared to talk about it with you or anyone else if it comes up. That's a good sign, isn't it, Doc? It means I'm handling things the right way?

I hope I didn't wait too long to say something about it, Doctor... is that alright?\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided talking about the accident with the ladder to anyone?\n\nPatient: Oh, no... no, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I don't go around bringing it up out of the blue, but that's mostly just because... well, I don't want to be a bother or bore folks with my troubles. I certainly don't want to be difficult or hide anything from you, Doctor. I just try my best not to complain too much to people. \n\nIs it alright that I handle it that way? I hope I'm giving you the right answers.\n\nDoctor: How upsetting are these memories of the accident when they come back to you?\n\nPatient: Well, I... Just for a second, it feels like I'm right back under there, waiting and hoping for someone to come help me. \n\nIt shakes me up a little bit, I suppose, but I always try to just take a deep breath and move on. I hope that doesn't sound too dramatic, Doctor. I'm really trying my best to handle it well... does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: How would you describe the level of distress you feel when these memories of the accident come back to you?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... I'm not rightly sure how to put a measurement on it, to be honest with you. 'Distress' sounds like a pretty strong word, and I... I promise I'm not falling apart or anything like that. \n\nIt's just... a bit bothersome, I suppose? I wouldn't call it a high level at all. I just get a little rattled for a moment and then I go find something to keep my hands busy. I really don't want to make a mountain out of a molehill here, so I wouldn't say it's anything too terrible. \n\nI hope that's an okay way to explain it... I just don't really know how to grade it for you, Doctor. Is that alright?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself trying not to think about the accident with the ladder?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say I actively try not to think about it. I mean, I don't sit there telling myself, \"Don't think about the ladder, don't think about the ladder.\" I just... I prefer to keep my mind on more pleasant things, you know? \n\nI've got my garden, and I like to watch the birds, so I just focus on that instead. It's not really avoiding it, it's just... choosing to be positive. I don't want to be the kind of person who dwells on the bad stuff. I try to be cheerful. \n\nDoes that sound like the right way to handle it, Doctor? I just want to make sure I'm doing what I'm supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel on edge or easily startled?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I wouldn't say I'm always on edge. I try to stay real steady, you know? I don't want you to think I'm just a nervous wreck all the time. \n\nBut, I suppose... well, if a door bangs shut real sudden-like, or if somebody comes up behind me without me hearing 'em... I do tend to jump pretty hard. And then my chest, it just keeps thumping and thumping for a good while. Even after I turn around and I know it's absolutely nothing to worry about, my heart just keeps racing. \n\nI always feel a bit silly when it happens, to be honest with you. I try to just laugh it off so folks don't think I'm getting frail or overly spooky. I hope that doesn't sound too bad, Doctor. It's just a reflex, I think... is that a normal thing to happen?\n\nDoctor: Do you try to avoid talking or thinking about what happened with the ladder?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying before, I don't really sit around trying not to think about it... but I suppose I have been, well, finding little excuses to stay away from certain spots. \n\nI just haven't felt much like going down to the senior center lately, and... well, I've been keeping out of the garage, too. Especially over by where the tools are, and... right around that corner where I used to keep the ladder. It's not that I'm scared or anything like that! I promise I'm not. It's just... I find other things that need doing inside the house instead. \n\nI just figure it's better to keep busy where I'm comfortable, rather than go looking for trouble. I just want to make sure I'm not letting things slip too much.\n\nDoctor: Do you try to avoid feeling the emotions that come up when you think about the accident with the ladder?\n\nPatient: Well, I...
